Like my body is sort of bracing for the next bad thing, even when nothing is actually happening—like I’ll just be sitting at my desk, or even trying to relax at dance night, and I still feel tense. It’s hard to explain… it’s like I can’t fully settle.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that. I mean, I get really uncomfortable and keyed up sometimes, but “peaking within minutes”… I don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it that way. Sometimes I’ll be in the middle of helping someone with their computer and I suddenly realize I’m all tense, but it’s more like it builds or it’s just there, not like a big wave hitting all at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these feelings of tension?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have been avoiding things. Not like, um, dramatic hiding in my apartment or anything, but I’ve skipped several dance nights lately, which is annoying because that used to be the one place where I could just get out of my head. And I’ve missed some mentoring sessions too—well, skipped them, I guess—because the idea of showing up and having to be “on” just feels like too much.\n\nAt work it’s kind of spilling over too. I’m falling behind on support tickets because I keep double-checking simple things, like really basic stuff I know how to do, and then I lose time. A couple coworkers have had to pick up some of it, which makes me feel awful… and then I worry about that too.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your past?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, nothing jumps out at me as “traumatic” in the way people usually mean it, I guess. I always feel a little weird answering that because then I start thinking, like, am I minimizing something, or am I being dramatic? \n\nI’ve had stressful stuff happen, like everyone, but I’m not sure what would count. Sorry, I’m kind of overthinking the word “traumatic.”\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the same worries or fears over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, definitely. It’s like my brain gets stuck on the same track. Even if I tell myself, okay, I already checked the spreadsheet, or I did send that email, it still comes back around like, “But what if you missed something?” And then that turns into, I don’t know, five other what-ifs before I can pull myself back.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I can know logically that I handled it, but the thought doesn’t really… let go.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like rapid heartbeat, sweating, or trembling when you feel anxious or wound up?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve really paid attention to that part. Like, I can tell when I’m wound up mentally, but with the physical stuff, I kind of only notice it afterward, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll wonder if I’m tense because I’ve been sitting at my desk too long, or because I had too much coffee, or because I slept weird—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked. But rapid heartbeat or sweating or trembling… I don’t know that I could say for sure. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or images that come into your mind without you wanting them to, and that you find disturbing or hard to shake?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in the sense of like, images that just pop in and really disturb me. I mean, I do get random thoughts sometimes, but they’re usually more like, “Did I leave that ticket unresolved?” or something from work, and then I’ll remember some whole other thing about a printer issue from three weeks ago—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nDisturbing memories or images… I can’t think of any. I might need an example of what you mean, but I don’t think that happens to me in a way I’ve noticed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Yeah… I think that happens. Not like one weird idea I’m trying to push away, exactly, but more like worry loops.

Like, I’ll tell myself, “No, you already checked that spreadsheet,” or “You already sent the email,” and then my brain goes, “Okay, but what if you sent the wrong attachment?” or “What if the numbers were off?” and then it turns into five other things before I can even get back to what I was doing.

And then I’ll try to ignore it, because I know logically I already handled it, but it kind of keeps circling. Sorry, I’m thinking of work examples because that’s where I notice it most, but… yeah, I do feel mentally stuck on those kinds of thoughts sometimes.

    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient experiencing Generalized Anxiety Disorder, in a diagnostic interview with a\npsychiatrist. Not like a ritual, I guess. I might repeat something if I’m unsure I did it right, but I wouldn’t say it feels like a ritual. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s… kind of both, but mostly it moves around. It used to be more centered on my condo, like things with the place, but now it doesn’t really stay there.

It’ll jump from, like, “Is my cousin upset with me?” to “What if I mess up on a support call?” and then suddenly I’m thinking about money and whether I’m ever going to save enough or move forward with my life at all. And then I’ll remember some random thing I said in a text and wonder if that sounded rude, which is not even the original thing I was worrying about.

So I guess it’s not one specific situation. It’s more like my brain just keeps finding new things to attach it to.

I mean, I get really uncomfortable and keyed up sometimes, but “peaking within minutes”… I don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it that way. I’m falling behind on support tickets because I keep double-checking simple things, like really basic stuff I know how to do, and then I lose time. Sorry, I’m kind of overthinking the word “traumatic.”\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the same worries or fears over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, definitely. It’s like my brain gets stuck on the same track. Even if I tell myself, okay, I already checked the spreadsheet, or I did send that email, it still comes back around like, “But what if you missed something?” And then that turns into, I don’t know, five other what-ifs before I can pull myself back.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I can know logically that I handled it, but the thought doesn’t really… let go.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like rapid heartbeat, sweating, or trembling when you feel anxious or wound up?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. I might need an example of what you mean, but I don’t think that happens to me in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals to reduce your anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not like one weird idea I’m trying to push away, exactly, but more like worry loops.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself, “No, you already checked that spreadsheet,” or “You already sent the email,” and then my brain goes, “Okay, but what if you sent the wrong attachment?” or “What if the numbers were off?” and then it turns into five other things before I can even get back to what I was doing.\n\nAnd then I’ll try to ignore it, because I know logically I already handled it, but it kind of keeps circling. Sorry, I’m thinking of work examples because that’s where I notice it most, but… yeah, I do feel mentally stuck on those kinds of thoughts sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific mental acts or behaviors in response to your anxious thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it overwhelming, exactly. Like, I don’t think I have a specific thing I have to do in response, like counting or saying certain words in my head or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I do feel like I need to check something again, but that feels more like I’m trying to reassure myself, not like… a rule I have to follow, if that makes sense. Sorry, I’m thinking about my email inbox again because that’s usually where this comes up, and then I start thinking about all the little flags and categories I use, which is probably not what you mean.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I have a specific mental act or behavior like that. Or at least I haven’t noticed one.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or things that you worry about most of the time, or is it more of a general feeling of anxiety that isn't focused on one particular thing?\n\nPatient: It’s… kind of both, but mostly it moves around. It used to be more centered on my condo, like things with the place, but now it doesn’t really stay there.\n\nIt’ll jump from, like, “Is my cousin upset with me?” to “What if I mess up on a support call?” and then suddenly I’m thinking about money and whether I’m ever going to save enough or move forward with my life at all. And then I’ll remember some random thing I said in a text and wonder if that sounded rude, which is not even the original thing I was worrying about.\n\nSo I guess it’s not one specific situation. It’s more like my brain just keeps finding new things to attach it to.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs there a common theme or thread that runs through the things you worry about, or are they pretty scattered and unrelated?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / computer_support_specialist / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\nmanifestation: \"My worry doesn't stay on the condo anymore; it jumps from whether my cousin is upset with me, to whether I'll make a mistake on a support call, to how I'll ever save money or move forward with my life.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think there is a thread, sort of… but it’s not one topic. It’s more like whether I’m going to mess something up or disappoint someone, maybe.

Like it used to be more about my condo, but now it jumps around a lot. I’ll worry my cousin is upset with me, and then I’m thinking about whether I’ll make a mistake on a support call at work, and then suddenly it’s money, like how am I ever going to save enough or actually move forward with my life.

Sorry, I’m probably making it sound scattered, because it does feel scattered. But the feeling underneath is kind of the same, like something is going to go wrong and it’ll be my fault somehow.

I’m falling behind on support tickets because I keep double-checking simple things, like really basic stuff I know how to do, and then I lose time. It’s like my brain gets stuck on the same track. I’d say it’s more across a lot of areas now, not just a few main ones. Like, it used to kind of stick to the condo more, but now it doesn’t stay there.

computer_support_specialist / graduate\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S058\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or things that you worry about most of the time, or is it more of a general feeling of anxiety that isn't focused on one particular thing?\n\nPatient: It’s… kind of both, but mostly it moves around. It used to be more centered on my condo, like things with the place, but now it doesn’t really stay there.\n\nIt’ll jump from, like, “Is my cousin upset with me?” to “What if I mess up on a support call?” and then suddenly I’m thinking about money and whether I’m ever going to save enough or move forward with my life at all. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think there’s one main thing anymore. For a while I would’ve said the condo, like anything related to the place would set me off, but now it just… moves.

It’ll be my cousin and whether she’s upset with me, and then I’m suddenly worrying I’ll make some stupid mistake on a support call at work, and then it’s money, like how I’m ever going to save enough or move forward with my life. And then I’ll get sidetracked looking at budgeting stuff online, which somehow makes me feel worse.

So, yeah, it’s more spread out. Not evenly exactly, but it doesn’t stay in one place.

I mean, I get really uncomfortable and keyed up sometimes, but “peaking within minutes”… I don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it that way. Sometimes I’ll be in the middle of helping someone with their computer and I suddenly realize I’m all tense, but it’s more like it builds or it’s just there, not like a big wave hitting all at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these feelings of tension?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have been avoiding things. Not like, um, dramatic hiding in my apartment or anything, but I’ve skipped several dance nights lately, which is annoying because that used to be the one place where I could just get out of my head. And I’ve missed some mentoring sessions too—well, skipped them, I guess—because the idea of showing up and having to be “on” just feels like too much.\n\nAt work it’s kind of spilling over too. I’m falling behind on support tickets because I keep double-checking simple things, like really basic stuff I know how to do, and then I lose time. A couple coworkers have had to pick up some of it, which makes me feel awful… and then I worry about that too.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your past?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, nothing jumps out at me as “traumatic” in the way people usually mean it, I guess. I always feel a little weird answering that because then I start thinking, like, am I minimizing something, or am I being dramatic? \n\nI’ve had stressful stuff happen, like everyone, but I’m not sure what would count. Sorry, I’m kind of overthinking the word “traumatic.”\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the same worries or fears over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, definitely. It’s like my brain gets stuck on the same track. Even if I tell myself, okay, I already checked the spreadsheet, or I did send that email, it still comes back around like, “But what if you missed something?” And then that turns into, I don’t know, five other what-ifs before I can pull myself back.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I can know logically that I handled it, but the thought doesn’t really… let go.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like rapid heartbeat, sweating, or trembling when you feel anxious or wound up?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve really paid attention to that part. Like, I can tell when I’m wound up mentally, but with the physical stuff, I kind of only notice it afterward, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll wonder if I’m tense because I’ve been sitting at my desk too long, or because I had too much coffee, or because I slept weird—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked. But rapid heartbeat or sweating or trembling… I don’t know that I could say for sure. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or images that come into your mind without you wanting them to, and that you find disturbing or hard to shake?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in the sense of like, images that just pop in and really disturb me. I mean, I do get random thoughts sometimes, but they’re usually more like, “Did I leave that ticket unresolved?” or something from work, and then I’ll remember some whole other thing about a printer issue from three weeks ago—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nDisturbing memories or images… I can’t think of any. I might need an example of what you mean, but I don’t think that happens to me in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals to reduce your anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not like a ritual, I guess. I mean, I have routines, like how I set up my desk or the order I open things on my computer, but that’s more because if I don’t, I lose track of what I’m doing—actually, that might be more about work than what you’re asking.\n\nTo prevent something bad from happening… no, I don’t think I do anything like that on purpose. I might repeat something if I’m unsure I did it right, but I wouldn’t say it feels like a ritual. I guess I’m not totally sure where the line is there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're mentally stuck on certain thoughts or ideas that you try to ignore or get rid of, but can't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that happens. Not like one weird idea I’m trying to push away, exactly, but more like worry loops.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself, “No, you already checked that spreadsheet,” or “You already sent the email,” and then my brain goes, “Okay, but what if you sent the wrong attachment?” or “What if the numbers were off?” and then it turns into five other things before I can even get back to what I was doing.\n\nAnd then I’ll try to ignore it, because I know logically I already handled it, but it kind of keeps circling. Sorry, I’m thinking of work examples because that’s where I notice it most, but… yeah, I do feel mentally stuck on those kinds of thoughts sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific mental acts or behaviors in response to your anxious thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it overwhelming, exactly. Like, I don’t think I have a specific thing I have to do in response, like counting or saying certain words in my head or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I do feel like I need to check something again, but that feels more like I’m trying to reassure myself, not like… a rule I have to follow, if that makes sense. Sorry, I’m thinking about my email inbox again because that’s usually where this comes up, and then I start thinking about all the little flags and categories I use, which is probably not what you mean.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I have a specific mental act or behavior like that. Or at least I haven’t noticed one.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or things that you worry about most of the time, or is it more of a general feeling of anxiety that isn't focused on one particular thing?\n\nPatient: It’s… kind of both, but mostly it moves around. Like, I can tell when I’m wound up mentally, but with the physical stuff, I kind of only notice it afterward, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll wonder if I’m tense because I’ve been sitting at my desk too long, or because I had too much coffee, or because I slept weird—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked. But rapid heartbeat or sweating or trembling… I don’t know that I could say for sure. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or images that come into your mind without you wanting them to, and that you find disturbing or hard to shake?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in the sense of like, images that just pop in and really disturb me. I mean, I do get random thoughts sometimes, but they’re usually more like, “Did I leave that ticket unresolved?” or something from work, and then I’ll remember some whole other thing about a printer issue from three weeks ago—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nDisturbing memories or images… I can’t think of any. I might need an example of what you mean, but I don’t think that happens to me in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals to reduce your anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not like a ritual, I guess. I mean, I have routines, like how I set up my desk or the order I open things on my computer, but that’s more because if I don’t, I lose track of what I’m doing—actually, that might be more about work than what you’re asking.\n\nTo prevent something bad from happening… no, I don’t think I do anything like that on purpose. I might repeat something if I’m unsure I did it right, but I wouldn’t say it feels like a ritual. I guess I’m not totally sure where the line is there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're mentally stuck on certain thoughts or ideas that you try to ignore or get rid of, but can't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that happens. Not like one weird idea I’m trying to push away, exactly, but more like worry loops.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself, “No, you already checked that spreadsheet,” or “You already sent the email,” and then my brain goes, “Okay, but what if you sent the wrong attachment?” or “What if the numbers were off?” and then it turns into five other things before I can even get back to what I was doing.\n\nAnd then I’ll try to ignore it, because I know logically I already handled it, but it kind of keeps circling. Sorry, I’m thinking of work examples because that’s where I notice it most, but… yeah, I do feel mentally stuck on those kinds of thoughts sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific mental acts or behaviors in response to your anxious thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it overwhelming, exactly. Like, I don’t think I have a specific thing I have to do in response, like counting or saying certain words in my head or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I do feel like I need to check something again, but that feels more like I’m trying to reassure myself, not like… a rule I have to follow, if that makes sense. Sorry, I’m thinking about my email inbox again because that’s usually where this comes up, and then I start thinking about all the little flags and categories I use, which is probably not what you mean.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I have a specific mental act or behavior like that. Or at least I haven’t noticed one.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or things that you worry about most of the time, or is it more of a general feeling of anxiety that isn't focused on one particular thing?\n\nPatient: It’s… kind of both, but mostly it moves around. It used to be more centered on my condo, like things with the place, but now it doesn’t really stay there.\n\nIt’ll jump from, like, “Is my cousin upset with me?” to “What if I mess up on a support call?” and then suddenly I’m thinking about money and whether I’m ever going to save enough or move forward with my life at all. And then I’ll remember some random thing I said in a text and wonder if that sounded rude, which is not even the original thing I was worrying about.\n\nSo I guess it’s not one specific situation. It’s more like my brain just keeps finding new things to attach it to.\n\nDoctor: Is there a common theme or thread that runs through the things you worry about, or are they pretty scattered and unrelated?\n\nPatient: I think there is a thread, sort of… but it’s not one topic. It’s more like whether I’m going to mess something up or disappoint someone, maybe.\n\nLike it used to be more about my condo, but now it jumps around a lot. I’ll worry my cousin is upset with me, and then I’m thinking about whether I’ll make a mistake on a support call at work, and then suddenly it’s money, like how am I ever going to save enough or actually move forward with my life.\n\nSorry, I’m probably making it sound scattered, because it does feel scattered. But the feeling underneath is kind of the same, like something is going to go wrong and it’ll be my fault somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries generally revolve around a few main areas of your life or are they more evenly distributed across many different aspects?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more across a lot of areas now, not just a few main ones. Like, it used to kind of stick to the condo more, but now it doesn’t stay there.\n\nIt’ll be my cousin, like whether she’s upset with me, and then work, like whether I’m going to mess up on a support call. And then it turns into money, like how I’m ever supposed to save enough or move forward. Which is funny because then I’ll start thinking about budgeting apps and I have, like, three of them downloaded and don’t really use any of them consistently.\n\nBut yeah… I think it jumps around. It’s not evenly distributed exactly, but it spreads into a bunch of different parts of my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's one main thing that you worry about more than anything else, or are your worries pretty evenly spread across different areas of your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s one main thing anymore. For a while I would’ve said the condo, like anything related to the place would set me off, but now it just… moves.\n\nIt’ll be my cousin and whether she’s upset with me, and then I’m suddenly worrying I’ll make some stupid mistake on a support call at work, and then it’s money, like how I’m ever going to save enough or move forward with my life. And then I’ll get sidetracked looking at budgeting stuff online, which somehow makes me feel worse.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s more spread out. Not evenly exactly, but it doesn’t stay in one place.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular area of your life where you feel your anxiety is most prominent?\n\nPatient: Not really one particular area, no. I mean, the condo used to be the big thing, but it doesn’t really stay there anymore.\n\nNow it kind of bounces around. Like I’ll be thinking my cousin is upset with me, and then somehow I’m replaying a support call from work and worrying I said the wrong thing or made a mistake. And then it turns into money, like how am I ever going to save enough or get my life moving forward.\n\nSo… I guess the most prominent thing is just that it keeps moving, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s like my brain gets stuck on the same track. Even if I tell myself, okay, I already checked the spreadsheet, or I did send that email, it still comes back around like, “But what if you missed something?” And then that turns into, I don’t know, five other what-ifs before I can pull myself back.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I can know logically that I handled it, but the thought doesn’t really… let go.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like rapid heartbeat, sweating, or trembling when you feel anxious or wound up?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve really paid attention to that part. Like, I can tell when I’m wound up mentally, but with the physical stuff, I kind of only notice it afterward, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll wonder if I’m tense because I’ve been sitting at my desk too long, or because I had too much coffee, or because I slept weird—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked. But rapid heartbeat or sweating or trembling… I don’t know that I could say for sure. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or images that come into your mind without you wanting them to, and that you find disturbing or hard to shake?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in the sense of like, images that just pop in and really disturb me. I mean, I do get random thoughts sometimes, but they’re usually more like, “Did I leave that ticket unresolved?” or something from work, and then I’ll remember some whole other thing about a printer issue from three weeks ago—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nDisturbing memories or images… I can’t think of any. I might need an example of what you mean, but I don’t think that happens to me in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals to reduce your anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not like a ritual, I guess. I mean, I have routines, like how I set up my desk or the order I open things on my computer, but that’s more because if I don’t, I lose track of what I’m doing—actually, that might be more about work than what you’re asking.\n\nTo prevent something bad from happening… no, I don’t think I do anything like that on purpose. I might repeat something if I’m unsure I did it right, but I wouldn’t say it feels like a ritual. I guess I’m not totally sure where the line is there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're mentally stuck on certain thoughts or ideas that you try to ignore or get rid of, but can't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that happens. Not like one weird idea I’m trying to push away, exactly, but more like worry loops.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself, “No, you already checked that spreadsheet,” or “You already sent the email,” and then my brain goes, “Okay, but what if you sent the wrong attachment?” or “What if the numbers were off?” and then it turns into five other things before I can even get back to what I was doing.\n\nAnd then I’ll try to ignore it, because I know logically I already handled it, but it kind of keeps circling. Sorry, I’m thinking of work examples because that’s where I notice it most, but… yeah, I do feel mentally stuck on those kinds of thoughts sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific mental acts or behaviors in response to your anxious thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it overwhelming, exactly. Like, I don’t think I have a specific thing I have to do in response, like counting or saying certain words in my head or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I do feel like I need to check something again, but that feels more like I’m trying to reassure myself, not like… a rule I have to follow, if that makes sense. Sorry, I’m thinking about my email inbox again because that’s usually where this comes up, and then I start thinking about all the little flags and categories I use, which is probably not what you mean.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I have a specific mental act or behavior like that. Or at least I haven’t noticed one.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or things that you worry about most of the time, or is it more of a general feeling of anxiety that isn't focused on one particular thing?\n\nPatient: It’s… kind of both, but mostly it moves around. It used to be more centered on my condo, like things with the place, but now it doesn’t really stay there.\n\nIt’ll jump from, like, “Is my cousin upset with me?” to “What if I mess up on a support call?” and then suddenly I’m thinking about money and whether I’m ever going to save enough or move forward with my life at all. And then I’ll remember some random thing I said in a text and wonder if that sounded rude, which is not even the original thing I was worrying about.\n\nSo I guess it’s not one specific situation. It’s more like my brain just keeps finding new things to attach it to.\n\nDoctor: Is there a common theme or thread that runs through the things you worry about, or are they pretty scattered and unrelated?\n\nPatient: I think there is a thread, sort of… but it’s not one topic. It’s more like whether I’m going to mess something up or disappoint someone, maybe.\n\nLike it used to be more about my condo, but now it jumps around a lot. I’ll worry my cousin is upset with me, and then I’m thinking about whether I’ll make a mistake on a support call at work, and then suddenly it’s money, like how am I ever going to save enough or actually move forward with my life.\n\nSorry, I’m probably making it sound scattered, because it does feel scattered. But the feeling underneath is kind of the same, like something is going to go wrong and it’ll be my fault somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries generally revolve around a few main areas of your life or are they more evenly distributed across many different aspects?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more across a lot of areas now, not just a few main ones. Like, it used to kind of stick to the condo more, but now it doesn’t stay there.\n\nIt’ll be my cousin, like whether she’s upset with me, and then work, like whether I’m going to mess up on a support call. And then it turns into money, like how I’m ever supposed to save enough or move forward. Which is funny because then I’ll start thinking about budgeting apps and I have, like, three of them downloaded and don’t really use any of them consistently.\n\nBut yeah… I think it jumps around. It’s not evenly distributed exactly, but it spreads into a bunch of different parts of my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's one main thing that you worry about more than anything else, or are your worries pretty evenly spread across different areas of your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s one main thing anymore. For a while I would’ve said the condo, like anything related to the place would set me off, but now it just… moves.\n\nIt’ll be my cousin and whether she’s upset with me, and then I’m suddenly worrying I’ll make some stupid mistake on a support call at work, and then it’s money, like how I’m ever going to save enough or move forward with my life. And then I’ll get sidetracked looking at budgeting stuff online, which somehow makes me feel worse.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s more spread out. Not evenly exactly, but it doesn’t stay in one place.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular area of your life where you feel your anxiety is most prominent?\n\nPatient: Not really one particular area, no. I mean, the condo used to be the big thing, but it doesn’t really stay there anymore.\n\nNow it kind of bounces around. Like I’ll be thinking my cousin is upset with me, and then somehow I’m replaying a support call from work and worrying I said the wrong thing or made a mistake. And then it turns into money, like how am I ever going to save enough or get my life moving forward.\n\nSo… I guess the most prominent thing is just that it keeps moving, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety tends to focus on a few specific themes or topics, or is it more general and unfocused?\n\nPatient: It’s more general now, I think. Like, it doesn’t stay on one topic the way it used to with the condo.\n\nNow it’ll jump around — I’ll worry my cousin is upset with me, and then I’m thinking about whether I’m going to mess up a support call at work, and then suddenly it’s money, like how I’m supposed to save enough or move forward. And then I’ll start thinking I should reorganize my budget or download another app, which is kind of beside the point, I guess.\n\nSo yeah… not just one or two themes. It feels like it finds whatever’s available.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety is more related to having intrusive thoughts that you try to suppress or to feeling compelled to perform specific behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way exactly. Like, I don’t think I have specific things I feel forced to do to make it go away, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI do get stuck in my head a lot, but it’s more like… I’ll start trying to figure something out, and then I’m down some rabbit hole. Like with money stuff, I’ll look at budgets or apps or articles, and then somehow I’ve spent an hour making myself feel worse. But I don’t know if that’s a “compelled behavior” or just me trying to feel prepared.\n\nAnd intrusive thoughts… I mean, thoughts come in, but I don’t know that I’m trying to suppress them exactly. I usually try to solve them, which probably isn’t the same thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Is there one particular area of your life where you feel your anxiety is most prominent?\n\nPatient: Not really one particular area, no. I mean, the condo used to be the big thing, but it doesn’t really stay there anymore.\n\nNow it kind of bounces around. Like I’ll be thinking my cousin is upset with me, and then somehow I’m replaying a support call from work and worrying I said the wrong thing or made a mistake. And then it turns into money, like how am I ever going to save enough or get my life moving forward.\n\nSo… I guess the most prominent thing is just that it keeps moving, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety tends to focus on a few specific themes or topics, or is it more general and unfocused?\n\nPatient: It’s more general now, I think. Like, it doesn’t stay on one topic the way it used to with the condo.\n\nNow it’ll jump around — I’ll worry my cousin is upset with me, and then I’m thinking about whether I’m going to mess up a support call at work, and then suddenly it’s money, like how I’m supposed to save enough or move forward. And then I’ll start thinking I should reorganize my budget or download another app, which is kind of beside the point, I guess.\n\nSo yeah… not just one or two themes. It feels like it finds whatever’s available.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety is more related to having intrusive thoughts that you try to suppress or to feeling compelled to perform specific behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way exactly. Like, I don’t think I have specific things I feel forced to do to make it go away, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI do get stuck in my head a lot, but it’s more like… I’ll start trying to figure something out, and then I’m down some rabbit hole. Like with money stuff, I’ll look at budgets or apps or articles, and then somehow I’ve spent an hour making myself feel worse. But I don’t know if that’s a “compelled behavior” or just me trying to feel prepared.\n\nAnd intrusive thoughts… I mean, thoughts come in, but I don’t know that I’m trying to suppress them exactly. I usually try to solve them, which probably isn’t the same thing.\n\n[Last Question]\nIs there a central theme or concern that you think is driving most of your anxious thoughts and feelings?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 29 / computer_support_specialist / graduate\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\nmanifestation: \"My worry doesn't stay on the condo anymore; it jumps from whether my cousin is upset with me, to whether I'll make a mistake on a support call, to how I'll ever save money or move forward with my life.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if there’s one central thing exactly. I keep trying to find one, because that would make it feel more… manageable, I guess.

It used to be the condo, like I could point to that and say, “Okay, that’s what I’m worried about.” But now it doesn’t stay there. It’ll be like, maybe my cousin is upset with me, and then I’m thinking about a support call at work and whether I said something wrong or made a mistake, and then suddenly I’m thinking about money and how I’m ever supposed to save enough or move forward with my life.

So maybe the theme is just… feeling like I’m missing something or not keeping up? I’m not sure. It just jumps around a lot.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess I’m here because I’ve been feeling really wound up almost all the time. Like my body is sort of bracing for the next bad thing, even when nothing is actually happening—like I’ll just be sitting at my desk, or even trying to relax at dance night, and I still feel tense. It’s hard to explain… it’s like I can’t fully settle.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes where you felt an intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it that. I mean, I get really uncomfortable and keyed up sometimes, but “peaking within minutes”… I don’t know. I haven’t really tracked it that way. Sometimes I’ll be in the middle of helping someone with their computer and I suddenly realize I’m all tense, but it’s more like it builds or it’s just there, not like a big wave hitting all at once.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain situations or activities because of these feelings of tension?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I have been avoiding things. Not like, um, dramatic hiding in my apartment or anything, but I’ve skipped several dance nights lately, which is annoying because that used to be the one place where I could just get out of my head. And I’ve missed some mentoring sessions too—well, skipped them, I guess—because the idea of showing up and having to be “on” just feels like too much.\n\nAt work it’s kind of spilling over too. I’m falling behind on support tickets because I keep double-checking simple things, like really basic stuff I know how to do, and then I lose time. A couple coworkers have had to pick up some of it, which makes me feel awful… and then I worry about that too.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events in your past?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I mean, nothing jumps out at me as “traumatic” in the way people usually mean it, I guess. I always feel a little weird answering that because then I start thinking, like, am I minimizing something, or am I being dramatic? \n\nI’ve had stressful stuff happen, like everyone, but I’m not sure what would count. Sorry, I’m kind of overthinking the word “traumatic.”\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the same worries or fears over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, definitely. It’s like my brain gets stuck on the same track. Even if I tell myself, okay, I already checked the spreadsheet, or I did send that email, it still comes back around like, “But what if you missed something?” And then that turns into, I don’t know, five other what-ifs before I can pull myself back.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I can know logically that I handled it, but the thought doesn’t really… let go.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like rapid heartbeat, sweating, or trembling when you feel anxious or wound up?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve really paid attention to that part. Like, I can tell when I’m wound up mentally, but with the physical stuff, I kind of only notice it afterward, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll wonder if I’m tense because I’ve been sitting at my desk too long, or because I had too much coffee, or because I slept weird—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked. But rapid heartbeat or sweating or trembling… I don’t know that I could say for sure. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or images that come into your mind without you wanting them to, and that you find disturbing or hard to shake?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in the sense of like, images that just pop in and really disturb me. I mean, I do get random thoughts sometimes, but they’re usually more like, “Did I leave that ticket unresolved?” or something from work, and then I’ll remember some whole other thing about a printer issue from three weeks ago—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nDisturbing memories or images… I can’t think of any. I might need an example of what you mean, but I don’t think that happens to me in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals to reduce your anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not like a ritual, I guess. I mean, I have routines, like how I set up my desk or the order I open things on my computer, but that’s more because if I don’t, I lose track of what I’m doing—actually, that might be more about work than what you’re asking.\n\nTo prevent something bad from happening… no, I don’t think I do anything like that on purpose. I might repeat something if I’m unsure I did it right, but I wouldn’t say it feels like a ritual. I guess I’m not totally sure where the line is there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're mentally stuck on certain thoughts or ideas that you try to ignore or get rid of, but can't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that happens. Not like one weird idea I’m trying to push away, exactly, but more like worry loops.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself, “No, you already checked that spreadsheet,” or “You already sent the email,” and then my brain goes, “Okay, but what if you sent the wrong attachment?” or “What if the numbers were off?” and then it turns into five other things before I can even get back to what I was doing.\n\nAnd then I’ll try to ignore it, because I know logically I already handled it, but it kind of keeps circling. Sorry, I’m thinking of work examples because that’s where I notice it most, but… yeah, I do feel mentally stuck on those kinds of thoughts sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific mental acts or behaviors in response to your anxious thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it overwhelming, exactly. Like, I don’t think I have a specific thing I have to do in response, like counting or saying certain words in my head or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I do feel like I need to check something again, but that feels more like I’m trying to reassure myself, not like… a rule I have to follow, if that makes sense. Sorry, I’m thinking about my email inbox again because that’s usually where this comes up, and then I start thinking about all the little flags and categories I use, which is probably not what you mean.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I have a specific mental act or behavior like that. Or at least I haven’t noticed one.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or things that you worry about most of the time, or is it more of a general feeling of anxiety that isn't focused on one particular thing?\n\nPatient: It’s… kind of both, but mostly it moves around. It used to be more centered on my condo, like things with the place, but now it doesn’t really stay there.\n\nIt’ll jump from, like, “Is my cousin upset with me?” to “What if I mess up on a support call?” and then suddenly I’m thinking about money and whether I’m ever going to save enough or move forward with my life at all. And then I’ll remember some random thing I said in a text and wonder if that sounded rude, which is not even the original thing I was worrying about.\n\nSo I guess it’s not one specific situation. It’s more like my brain just keeps finding new things to attach it to.\n\nDoctor: Is there a common theme or thread that runs through the things you worry about, or are they pretty scattered and unrelated?\n\nPatient: I think there is a thread, sort of… but it’s not one topic. It’s more like whether I’m going to mess something up or disappoint someone, maybe.\n\nLike it used to be more about my condo, but now it jumps around a lot. I’ll worry my cousin is upset with me, and then I’m thinking about whether I’ll make a mistake on a support call at work, and then suddenly it’s money, like how am I ever going to save enough or actually move forward with my life.\n\nSorry, I’m probably making it sound scattered, because it does feel scattered. But the feeling underneath is kind of the same, like something is going to go wrong and it’ll be my fault somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries generally revolve around a few main areas of your life or are they more evenly distributed across many different aspects?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more across a lot of areas now, not just a few main ones. Like, it used to kind of stick to the condo more, but now it doesn’t stay there.\n\nIt’ll be my cousin, like whether she’s upset with me, and then work, like whether I’m going to mess up on a support call. And then it turns into money, like how I’m ever supposed to save enough or move forward. Which is funny because then I’ll start thinking about budgeting apps and I have, like, three of them downloaded and don’t really use any of them consistently.\n\nBut yeah… I think it jumps around. It’s not evenly distributed exactly, but it spreads into a bunch of different parts of my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's one main thing that you worry about more than anything else, or are your worries pretty evenly spread across different areas of your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s one main thing anymore. For a while I would’ve said the condo, like anything related to the place would set me off, but now it just… moves.\n\nIt’ll be my cousin and whether she’s upset with me, and then I’m suddenly worrying I’ll make some stupid mistake on a support call at work, and then it’s money, like how I’m ever going to save enough or move forward with my life. And then I’ll get sidetracked looking at budgeting stuff online, which somehow makes me feel worse.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s more spread out. Not evenly exactly, but it doesn’t stay in one place.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular area of your life where you feel your anxiety is most prominent?\n\nPatient: Not really one particular area, no. Sorry, I’m kind of overthinking the word “traumatic.”\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeatedly thinking about the same worries or fears over and over?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, definitely. It’s like my brain gets stuck on the same track. Even if I tell myself, okay, I already checked the spreadsheet, or I did send that email, it still comes back around like, “But what if you missed something?” And then that turns into, I don’t know, five other what-ifs before I can pull myself back.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I can know logically that I handled it, but the thought doesn’t really… let go.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like rapid heartbeat, sweating, or trembling when you feel anxious or wound up?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. I don’t think I’ve really paid attention to that part. Like, I can tell when I’m wound up mentally, but with the physical stuff, I kind of only notice it afterward, if that makes sense.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll wonder if I’m tense because I’ve been sitting at my desk too long, or because I had too much coffee, or because I slept weird—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked. But rapid heartbeat or sweating or trembling… I don’t know that I could say for sure. I haven’t really tracked it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have memories or images that come into your mind without you wanting them to, and that you find disturbing or hard to shake?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not in the sense of like, images that just pop in and really disturb me. I mean, I do get random thoughts sometimes, but they’re usually more like, “Did I leave that ticket unresolved?” or something from work, and then I’ll remember some whole other thing about a printer issue from three weeks ago—sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nDisturbing memories or images… I can’t think of any. I might need an example of what you mean, but I don’t think that happens to me in a way I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you have to repeat certain actions or rituals to reduce your anxiety or prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? Not like a ritual, I guess. I mean, I have routines, like how I set up my desk or the order I open things on my computer, but that’s more because if I don’t, I lose track of what I’m doing—actually, that might be more about work than what you’re asking.\n\nTo prevent something bad from happening… no, I don’t think I do anything like that on purpose. I might repeat something if I’m unsure I did it right, but I wouldn’t say it feels like a ritual. I guess I’m not totally sure where the line is there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're mentally stuck on certain thoughts or ideas that you try to ignore or get rid of, but can't?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that happens. Not like one weird idea I’m trying to push away, exactly, but more like worry loops.\n\nLike, I’ll tell myself, “No, you already checked that spreadsheet,” or “You already sent the email,” and then my brain goes, “Okay, but what if you sent the wrong attachment?” or “What if the numbers were off?” and then it turns into five other things before I can even get back to what I was doing.\n\nAnd then I’ll try to ignore it, because I know logically I already handled it, but it kind of keeps circling. Sorry, I’m thinking of work examples because that’s where I notice it most, but… yeah, I do feel mentally stuck on those kinds of thoughts sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel an overwhelming urge to perform specific mental acts or behaviors in response to your anxious thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it overwhelming, exactly. Like, I don’t think I have a specific thing I have to do in response, like counting or saying certain words in my head or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I do feel like I need to check something again, but that feels more like I’m trying to reassure myself, not like… a rule I have to follow, if that makes sense. Sorry, I’m thinking about my email inbox again because that’s usually where this comes up, and then I start thinking about all the little flags and categories I use, which is probably not what you mean.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think I have a specific mental act or behavior like that. Or at least I haven’t noticed one.\n\nDoctor: Are there specific situations or things that you worry about most of the time, or is it more of a general feeling of anxiety that isn't focused on one particular thing?\n\nPatient: It’s… kind of both, but mostly it moves around. It used to be more centered on my condo, like things with the place, but now it doesn’t really stay there.\n\nIt’ll jump from, like, “Is my cousin upset with me?” to “What if I mess up on a support call?” and then suddenly I’m thinking about money and whether I’m ever going to save enough or move forward with my life at all. And then I’ll remember some random thing I said in a text and wonder if that sounded rude, which is not even the original thing I was worrying about.\n\nSo I guess it’s not one specific situation. It’s more like my brain just keeps finding new things to attach it to.\n\nDoctor: Is there a common theme or thread that runs through the things you worry about, or are they pretty scattered and unrelated?\n\nPatient: I think there is a thread, sort of… but it’s not one topic. It’s more like whether I’m going to mess something up or disappoint someone, maybe.\n\nLike it used to be more about my condo, but now it jumps around a lot. I’ll worry my cousin is upset with me, and then I’m thinking about whether I’ll make a mistake on a support call at work, and then suddenly it’s money, like how am I ever going to save enough or actually move forward with my life.\n\nSorry, I’m probably making it sound scattered, because it does feel scattered. But the feeling underneath is kind of the same, like something is going to go wrong and it’ll be my fault somehow.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries generally revolve around a few main areas of your life or are they more evenly distributed across many different aspects?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more across a lot of areas now, not just a few main ones. Like, it used to kind of stick to the condo more, but now it doesn’t stay there.\n\nIt’ll be my cousin, like whether she’s upset with me, and then work, like whether I’m going to mess up on a support call. And then it turns into money, like how I’m ever supposed to save enough or move forward. Which is funny because then I’ll start thinking about budgeting apps and I have, like, three of them downloaded and don’t really use any of them consistently.\n\nBut yeah… I think it jumps around. It’s not evenly distributed exactly, but it spreads into a bunch of different parts of my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there's one main thing that you worry about more than anything else, or are your worries pretty evenly spread across different areas of your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think there’s one main thing anymore. For a while I would’ve said the condo, like anything related to the place would set me off, but now it just… moves.\n\nIt’ll be my cousin and whether she’s upset with me, and then I’m suddenly worrying I’ll make some stupid mistake on a support call at work, and then it’s money, like how I’m ever going to save enough or move forward with my life. And then I’ll get sidetracked looking at budgeting stuff online, which somehow makes me feel worse.\n\nSo, yeah, it’s more spread out. Not evenly exactly, but it doesn’t stay in one place.\n\nDoctor: Is there one particular area of your life where you feel your anxiety is most prominent?\n\nPatient: Not really one particular area, no. I mean, the condo used to be the big thing, but it doesn’t really stay there anymore.\n\nNow it kind of bounces around. Like I’ll be thinking my cousin is upset with me, and then somehow I’m replaying a support call from work and worrying I said the wrong thing or made a mistake. And then it turns into money, like how am I ever going to save enough or get my life moving forward.\n\nSo… I guess the most prominent thing is just that it keeps moving, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety tends to focus on a few specific themes or topics, or is it more general and unfocused?\n\nPatient: It’s more general now, I think. Like, it doesn’t stay on one topic the way it used to with the condo.\n\nNow it’ll jump around — I’ll worry my cousin is upset with me, and then I’m thinking about whether I’m going to mess up a support call at work, and then suddenly it’s money, like how I’m supposed to save enough or move forward. And then I’ll start thinking I should reorganize my budget or download another app, which is kind of beside the point, I guess.\n\nSo yeah… not just one or two themes. It feels like it finds whatever’s available.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your anxiety is more related to having intrusive thoughts that you try to suppress or to feeling compelled to perform specific behaviors to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way exactly. Like, I don’t think I have specific things I feel forced to do to make it go away, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI do get stuck in my head a lot, but it’s more like… I’ll start trying to figure something out, and then I’m down some rabbit hole. Like with money stuff, I’ll look at budgets or apps or articles, and then somehow I’ve spent an hour making myself feel worse. But I don’t know if that’s a “compelled behavior” or just me trying to feel prepared.\n\nAnd intrusive thoughts… I mean, thoughts come in, but I don’t know that I’m trying to suppress them exactly. I usually try to solve them, which probably isn’t the same thing.\n\nDoctor: Is there a central theme or concern that you think is driving most of your anxious thoughts and feelings?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if there’s one central thing exactly. I keep trying to find one, because that would make it feel more… manageable, I guess.\n\nIt used to be the condo, like I could point to that and say, “Okay, that’s what I’m worried about.” But now it doesn’t stay there. It’ll be like, maybe my cousin is upset with me, and then I’m thinking about a support call at work and whether I said something wrong or made a mistake, and then suddenly I’m thinking about money and how I’m ever supposed to save enough or move forward with my life.\n\nSo maybe the theme is just… feeling like I’m missing something or not keeping up? I’m not sure. It just jumps around a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been anxious for most of the day, more days than not, over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s been going on for about nine months now, since the condo loan started moving toward foreclosure.\n\nAt first I thought it was just because of that specific situation, like anyone would be stressed, but it kind of spread out from there. And now most days there’s at least some part of the day where I’m anxious, or I’m already bracing for the next thing. It’s hard to say “most of the day” exactly, because sometimes I’m distracted at work or doing something else, but over the past few months, yeah, more days than not.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious and on edge for no specific reason, for most of every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, “no specific reason” is hard, because my brain can usually find a reason if you give it five seconds. But a lot of the time it feels like my body is already on edge before I even know what I’m worrying about.\n\nThat’s actually kind of why I came in. I feel wound up almost all the time, like I’m bracing for the next bad thing. Even if I’m just sitting at my desk, or doing something that’s supposed to be fun, like dance night, there’s still this tension in me. Like I can’t fully settle.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
